Key Design Decisions Summary
Concern	Solution	Why
Service Discovery	Eureka	Dynamic routing, load balancing
Client-side LB	Spring Cloud LoadBalancer	Distribute load across instances
Resilience	Resilience4j	Circuit breakers, retries, timeouts
Service Calls	Feign Client	Declarative REST, integration with LB
API Gateway	Spring Cloud Gateway	Routing, filtering, rate limiting
Security	JWT + Spring Security	Stateless auth, scalable
Database	PostgreSQL + HikariCP	Reliable, connection pooling
Caching	Caffeine	Low latency, reduced DB load
API Versioning	URI versioning (/v1/)	Simple, cache-friendly
Error Handling	Global @ControllerAdvice	Consistent error responses
Monitoring	Actuator + Prometheus	Production readiness
This architecture gives you:

High performance with connection pooling and caching

Resilience with circuit breakers and retries

Scalability through horizontal scaling and service discovery

Maintainability through clear separation of concerns

Production readiness with comprehensive monitoring

The system remains Java 8 compatible while using modern Spring Boot patterns!
=======
How to compose microservices together?
Excellent question! Service composition is the art of combining multiple microservices to fulfill business workflows. Let me show you the 4 main composition patterns with Java 8, Spring Boot, and practical implementations.

The 4 Composition Patterns
text
1. Orchestration    2. Choreography     3. Aggregation     4. Saga (Hybrid)
   (Conductor)         (Dance)            (Data)            (Transactions)
Pattern 1: Orchestration (API Gateway Pattern)
Best for: Complex workflows, centralized control, when you need request/response

Implementation: Order Orchestrator Service
java
@RestController
@RequestMapping("/api/v1/orders")
@Slf4j
public class OrderOrchestratorController {
    
    private final OrderOrchestratorService orchestrator;
    
    @PostMapping
    public ResponseEntity<OrderResponse> createOrder(@Valid @RequestBody CreateOrderRequest request) {
        // Orchestrator coordinates multiple services
        OrderResponse response = orchestrator.executeOrderWorkflow(request);
        return ResponseEntity.accepted().body(response);
    }
}

@Service
@Slf4j
public class OrderOrchestratorService {
    
    private final CustomerClient customerClient;
    private final ProductClient productClient;
    private final InventoryClient inventoryClient;
    private final PaymentClient paymentClient;
    private final ShippingClient shippingClient;
    private final OrderRepository orderRepository;
    
    @Transactional
    public OrderResponse executeOrderWorkflow(CreateOrderRequest request) {
        
        // Step 1: Validate Customer
        CustomerDTO customer = customerClient.getCustomer(request.getCustomerId())
            .orElseThrow(() -> new BusinessException("Customer not found"));
        
        // Step 2: Get Product Details
        ProductDTO product = productClient.getProduct(request.getProductId())
            .orElseThrow(() -> new BusinessException("Product not found"));
        
        // Step 3: Check Inventory
        InventoryResponse inventory = inventoryClient.checkStock(request.getProductId(), request.getQuantity());
        if (!inventory.isAvailable()) {
            throw new InsufficientStockException("Product out of stock");
        }
        
        // Step 4: Create Order (local)
        Order order = createLocalOrder(request, customer, product);
        
        try {
            // Step 5: Reserve Inventory
            ReservationResponse reservation = inventoryClient.reserveStock(
                request.getProductId(), request.getQuantity(), order.getId()
            );
            
            // Step 6: Process Payment
            PaymentResponse payment = paymentClient.processPayment(
                order.getId(), customer.getId(), order.getTotalAmount()
            );
            
            // Step 7: Create Shipment
            ShipmentResponse shipment = shippingClient.createShipment(
                order.getId(), customer.getAddress(), product.getSku(), request.getQuantity()
            );
            
            // Step 8: Update Order Status
            order.setStatus(OrderStatus.CONFIRMED);
            order.setShipmentId(shipment.getShipmentId());
            order.setPaymentId(payment.getTransactionId());
            orderRepository.save(order);
            
            return OrderResponse.success(order);
            
        } catch (Exception e) {
            // Compensating transactions
            log.error("Order failed, initiating rollback", e);
            rollbackOrder(order, e);
            throw new OrderProcessingException("Order processing failed", e);
        }
    }
    
    private void rollbackOrder(Order order, Exception cause) {
        // Compensate each step
        try {
            if (order.getPaymentId() != null) {
                paymentClient.refundPayment(order.getPaymentId());
            }
            if (order.getReservationId() != null) {
                inventoryClient.releaseReservation(order.getReservationId());
            }
            order.setStatus(OrderStatus.FAILED);
            order.setFailureReason(cause.getMessage());
            orderRepository.save(order);
        } catch (Exception rollbackError) {
            log.error("Rollback failed for order: {}", order.getId(), rollbackError);
            // Alert for manual intervention
        }
    }
    
    private Order createLocalOrder(CreateOrderRequest request, CustomerDTO customer, ProductDTO product) {
        Order order = new Order();
        order.setCustomerId(customer.getId());
        order.setProductId(product.getId());
        order.setQuantity(request.getQuantity());
        order.setTotalAmount(product.getPrice().multiply(BigDecimal.valueOf(request.getQuantity())));
        order.setStatus(OrderStatus.PENDING);
        return orderRepository.save(order);
    }
}
Orchestrator with Async + CompletableFuture (Java 8)
java
@Service
@Slf4j
public class AsyncOrderOrchestrator {
    
    @Autowired
    private ThreadPoolTaskExecutor executor;
    
    public CompletableFuture<OrderResponse> orchestrateAsync(CreateOrderRequest request) {
        
        // Parallel calls for independent validations
        CompletableFuture<CustomerDTO> customerFuture = 
            CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> customerClient.getCustomer(request.getCustomerId()), executor);
        
        CompletableFuture<ProductDTO> productFuture = 
            CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> productClient.getProduct(request.getProductId()), executor);
        
        // Combine results
        return CompletableFuture
            .allOf(customerFuture, productFuture)
            .thenApplyAsync(v -> {
                CustomerDTO customer = customerFuture.join();
                ProductDTO product = productFuture.join();
                
                // Sequential dependent steps
                Order order = createOrder(customer, product, request);
                paymentClient.processPayment(order);
                inventoryClient.reserveStock(order);
                shippingClient.createShipment(order);
                
                return OrderResponse.success(order);
            }, executor)
            .exceptionally(throwable -> {
                log.error("Orchestration failed", throwable);
                return OrderResponse.failed(throwable.getMessage());
            });
    }
}
Pattern 2: Choreography (Event-Driven Composition)
Best for: Decoupled systems, eventual consistency, complex event flows

Implementation with Spring Events + Kafka
java
// Domain Events
public class OrderPlacedEvent {
    private final UUID orderId;
    private final UUID customerId;
    private final List<OrderLine> items;
    private final BigDecimal totalAmount;
    private final LocalDateTime occurredAt;
    
    // constructor, getters
}

// Order Service - Publishes Event
@Service
@Slf4j
public class OrderService {
    
    @Autowired
    private ApplicationEventPublisher eventPublisher;
    
    @Autowired
    private KafkaTemplate<String, Object> kafkaTemplate;
    
    @Transactional
    public Order placeOrder(CreateOrderRequest request) {
        Order order = createOrder(request);
        
        // Publish event (both local and Kafka for reliability)
        OrderPlacedEvent event = new OrderPlacedEvent(order);
        
        // Local Spring event for immediate consumers
        eventPublisher.publishEvent(event);
        
        // Kafka for cross-service communication
        kafkaTemplate.send("order.events", "order.placed", event);
        
        return order;
    }
}

// Inventory Service - Reacts to OrderPlacedEvent
@Service
@Slf4j
public class InventoryEventHandler {
    
    @Autowired
    private InventoryService inventoryService;
    
    @Autowired
    private KafkaTemplate<String, Object> kafkaTemplate;
    
    @EventListener
    @Async
    @Transactional
    public void handleOrderPlaced(OrderPlacedEvent event) {
        log.info("Inventory service handling order placed: {}", event.getOrderId());
        
        try {
            // Reserve stock
            boolean reserved = inventoryService.reserveStock(event.getItems());
            
            if (reserved) {
                // Publish success event
                StockReservedEvent stockEvent = new StockReservedEvent(event.getOrderId(), event.getItems());
                kafkaTemplate.send("inventory.events", "stock.reserved", stockEvent);
            } else {
                // Publish failure event
                StockReservationFailedEvent failedEvent = new StockReservationFailedEvent(
                    event.getOrderId(), "Insufficient stock"
                );
                kafkaTemplate.send("inventory.events", "stock.reservation.failed", failedEvent);
            }
        } catch (Exception e) {
            log.error("Failed to reserve stock", e);
            kafkaTemplate.send("inventory.events", "stock.reservation.failed", 
                new StockReservationFailedEvent(event.getOrderId(), e.getMessage()));
        }
    }
}

// Payment Service - Reacts to StockReservedEvent
@Service
@Slf4j
public class PaymentEventHandler {
    
    @KafkaListener(topics = "inventory.events", groupId = "payment-group")
    public void handleStockReserved(StockReservedEvent event) {
        log.info("Payment service processing: {}", event.getOrderId());
        
        try {
            Payment payment = paymentService.processPayment(event.getOrderId(), event.getTotalAmount());
            
            PaymentProcessedEvent paymentEvent = new PaymentProcessedEvent(
                event.getOrderId(), payment.getTransactionId(), true
            );
            kafkaTemplate.send("payment.events", "payment.processed", paymentEvent);
            
        } catch (Exception e) {
            log.error("Payment failed", e);
            kafkaTemplate.send("payment.events", "payment.failed", 
                new PaymentFailedEvent(event.getOrderId(), e.getMessage()));
        }
    }
}

// Shipping Service - Reacts to PaymentProcessedEvent
@Service
@Slf4j
public class ShippingEventHandler {
    
    @KafkaListener(topics = "payment.events", groupId = "shipping-group")
    public void handlePaymentProcessed(PaymentProcessedEvent event) {
        if (event.isSuccess()) {
            log.info("Shipping service creating shipment for order: {}", event.getOrderId());
            shippingService.createShipment(event.getOrderId());
        } else {
            log.warn("Payment failed, not shipping order: {}", event.getOrderId());
            // Could send compensation event back to order service
        }
    }
}

Saga Choreography with Compensation Events

java
// Saga Coordinator using Choreography
@Component
public class OrderSagaChoreography {
    
    // Each service emits events that trigger the next step
    
    // Order Service
    @EventListener
    @Async
    public void startSaga(OrderPlacedEvent event) {
        // Emit: ORDER_PLACED
    }
    
    // Inventory Service
    @EventListener
    @Async
    public void onOrderPlaced(OrderPlacedEvent event) {
        // Process and emit: INVENTORY_RESERVED or INVENTORY_FAILED
    }
    
    // Payment Service
    @EventListener
    @Async
    public void onInventoryReserved(InventoryReservedEvent event) {
        // Process and emit: PAYMENT_PROCESSED or PAYMENT_FAILED
    }
    
    // Compensation: Payment Service
    @EventListener
    @Async
    public void onInventoryFailed(InventoryFailedEvent event) {
        // Emit: ORDER_CANCELLED (no payment yet, so no compensation needed)
    }
    
    // Compensation: Inventory Service
    @EventListener
    @Async
    public void onPaymentFailed(PaymentFailedEvent event) {
        // Emit: INVENTORY_RELEASE (compensation)
        kafkaTemplate.send("inventory.commands", "release.stock", event);
    }
}
Pattern 3: Aggregation (API Composition)
Best for: Dashboard views, data gathering, read-side optimization

Implementation: Dashboard Aggregator Service
java
@RestController
@RequestMapping("/api/v1/dashboard")
@Slf4j
public class DashboardAggregatorController {
    
    private final DashboardAggregatorService aggregator;
    
    @GetMapping("/customer/{customerId}")
    public CompletableFuture<CustomerDashboardResponse> getCustomerDashboard(@PathVariable UUID customerId) {
        return aggregator.aggregateCustomerDashboard(customerId);
    }
}

@Service
@Slf4j
public class DashboardAggregatorService {
    
    private final CustomerClient customerClient;
    private final OrderClient orderClient;
    private final PaymentClient paymentClient;
    private final ShippingClient shippingClient;
    private final RecommendationClient recommendationClient;
    
    @Autowired
    private ThreadPoolTaskExecutor executor;
    
    public CompletableFuture<CustomerDashboardResponse> aggregateCustomerDashboard(UUID customerId) {
        
        // Parallel calls to multiple services
        CompletableFuture<CustomerProfile> profileFuture = 
            CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> customerClient.getProfile(customerId), executor);
        
        CompletableFuture<List<OrderSummary>> ordersFuture = 
            CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> orderClient.getCustomerOrders(customerId), executor);
        
        CompletableFuture<PaymentSummary> paymentsFuture = 
            CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> paymentClient.getCustomerPayments(customerId), executor);
        
        CompletableFuture<List<Shipment>> shipmentsFuture = 
            CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> shippingClient.getCustomerShipments(customerId), executor);
        
        CompletableFuture<List<Product>> recommendationsFuture = 
            CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> recommendationClient.getRecommendations(customerId), executor);
        
        // Combine all results
        return CompletableFuture
            .allOf(profileFuture, ordersFuture, paymentsFuture, shipmentsFuture, recommendationsFuture)
            .thenApplyAsync(v -> {
                CustomerDashboardResponse response = new CustomerDashboardResponse();
                response.setProfile(profileFuture.join());
                response.setOrders(ordersFuture.join());
                response.setPayments(paymentsFuture.join());
                response.setShipments(shipmentsFuture.join());
                response.setRecommendations(recommendationsFuture.join());
                response.setAggregatedAt(LocalDateTime.now());
                
                // Calculate derived metrics
                response.setTotalOrders(response.getOrders().size());
                response.setTotalSpent(calculateTotalSpent(response.getPayments()));
                response.setActiveShipments(countActiveShipments(response.getShipments()));
                
                return response;
            }, executor)
            .exceptionally(throwable -> {
                log.error("Dashboard aggregation failed for customer: {}", customerId, throwable);
                // Return partial data if possible
                return getPartialDashboard(customerId, profileFuture, ordersFuture, throwable);
            });
    }
    
    private BigDecimal calculateTotalSpent(PaymentSummary payments) {
        return payments.getCompletedPayments().stream()
            .map(Payment::getAmount)
            .reduce(BigDecimal.ZERO, BigDecimal::add);
    }
}
Resilient Aggregator with Timeouts & Fallbacks
java
@Service
@Slf4j
public class ResilientAggregator {
    
    @CircuitBreaker(name = "customer-service", fallbackMethod = "getCachedProfile")
    public CustomerProfile getCustomerProfile(UUID customerId) {
        return customerClient.getProfile(customerId);
    }
    
    @CircuitBreaker(name = "order-service", fallbackMethod = "getEmptyOrders")
    public List<OrderSummary> getCustomerOrders(UUID customerId) {
        return orderClient.getCustomerOrders(customerId);
    }
    
    private CustomerProfile getCachedProfile(UUID customerId, Exception ex) {
        log.warn("Customer service unavailable, using cached profile for: {}", customerId);
        return cacheService.getCustomerProfile(customerId);
    }
    
    private List<OrderSummary> getEmptyOrders(UUID customerId, Exception ex) {
        log.warn("Order service unavailable for customer: {}", customerId);
        return Collections.emptyList();
    }
}
Pattern 4: Saga Pattern (Transaction Composition)
Best for: Distributed transactions, compensating actions

Orchestrated Saga Implementation
java
// Saga Orchestrator with Step Registry
@Component
@Slf4j
public class OrderSagaOrchestrator {
    
    private final SagaStepRegistry stepRegistry;
    private final SagaStateRepository stateRepository;
    private final KafkaTemplate<String, Object> kafkaTemplate;
    
    @Autowired
    private ThreadPoolTaskExecutor executor;
    
    public CompletableFuture<SagaResult> executeSaga(CreateOrderRequest request) {
        String sagaId = UUID.randomUUID().toString();
        SagaState state = new SagaState(sagaId, request);
        stateRepository.save(state);
        
        return executeStep(state, 0);
    }
    
    private CompletableFuture<SagaResult> executeStep(SagaState state, int stepIndex) {
        if (stepIndex >= stepRegistry.getSteps().size()) {
            // Saga completed successfully
            state.setStatus(SagaStatus.COMPLETED);
            stateRepository.save(state);
            return CompletableFuture.completedFuture(SagaResult.success(state));
        }
        
        SagaStep step = stepRegistry.getSteps().get(stepIndex);
        
        return step.execute(state)
            .thenCompose(result -> {
                if (result.isSuccess()) {
                    state.addExecutedStep(step.getName());
                    stateRepository.save(state);
                    return executeStep(state, stepIndex + 1);
                } else {
                    // Step failed, start compensation
                    return compensate(state, stepIndex - 1);
                }
            })
            .exceptionally(throwable -> {
                log.error("Saga step failed: {}", step.getName(), throwable);
                return compensate(state, stepIndex - 1).join();
            });
    }
    
    private CompletableFuture<SagaResult> compensate(SagaState state, int stepIndex) {
        state.setStatus(SagaStatus.COMPENSATING);
        stateRepository.save(state);
        
        // Execute compensation in reverse order
        CompletableFuture<Void> compensation = CompletableFuture.completedFuture(null);
        
        for (int i = stepIndex; i >= 0; i--) {
            final int currentStep = i;
            compensation = compensation.thenCompose(v -> {
                SagaStep step = stepRegistry.getSteps().get(currentStep);
                return step.compensate(state);
            });
        }
        
        return compensation.thenApply(v -> {
            state.setStatus(SagaStatus.FAILED);
            stateRepository.save(state);
            return SagaResult.failed(state, "Saga failed at step: " + stepRegistry.getSteps().get(stepIndex + 1).getName());
        });
    }
}

// Individual Saga Step
@Component
public class ReserveInventoryStep implements SagaStep {
    
    private final InventoryClient inventoryClient;
    
    @Override
    public CompletableFuture<StepResult> execute(SagaState state) {
        CreateOrderRequest request = state.getRequest();
        
        return CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> {
            ReservationResponse response = inventoryClient.reserveStock(
                request.getProductId(), request.getQuantity(), state.getSagaId()
            );
            
            if (response.isSuccess()) {
                state.setReservationId(response.getReservationId());
                return StepResult.success();
            } else {
                return StepResult.failed(response.getMessage());
            }
        });
    }
    
    @Override
    public CompletableFuture<Void> compensate(SagaState state) {
        if (state.getReservationId() != null) {
            return CompletableFuture.runAsync(() -> {
                inventoryClient.releaseReservation(state.getReservationId());
                log.info("Released reservation: {}", state.getReservationId());
            });
        }
        return CompletableFuture.completedFuture(null);
    }
}
Comparison & Selection Guide
Pattern	Coupling	Latency	Complexity	Best For
Orchestration	Tight (Central coordinator)	Higher (Sequential)	Medium	Complex workflows, need response
Choreography	Loose (Events)	Lower (Async)	High	Decoupled systems, event-driven
Aggregation	Loose (Read-only)	Parallel (Lower)	Low	Dashboards, data composition
Saga	Medium	Variable	High	Distributed transactions
Hybrid Approach: Best of Both Worlds
java
@Component
public class HybridOrderProcessor {
    
    // Use orchestration for critical path
    public OrderResponse processOrderSync(CreateOrderRequest request) {
        // Synchronous orchestration for immediate response
        return orchestrator.executeOrderWorkflow(request);
    }
    
    // Use choreography for side effects
    @EventListener
    @Async
    public void handleOrderConfirmed(OrderConfirmedEvent event) {
        // Async events for notifications, analytics, etc.
        kafkaTemplate.send("order.notifications", event);
        kafkaTemplate.send("order.analytics", event);
        kafkaTemplate.send("order.recommendations", event);
    }
    
    // Use aggregation for queries
    public DashboardResponse getOrderDashboard(UUID orderId) {
        return aggregator.aggregateOrderData(orderId);
    }
}
Configuration for Thread Pools (Java 8)
java
@Configuration
@EnableAsync
public class AsyncConfig {
    
    @Bean(name = "orchestratorExecutor")
    public ThreadPoolTaskExecutor orchestratorExecutor() {
        ThreadPoolTaskExecutor executor = new ThreadPoolTaskExecutor();
        executor.setCorePoolSize(10);
        executor.setMaxPoolSize(50);
        executor.setQueueCapacity(100);
        executor.setThreadNamePrefix("orchestrator-");
        executor.setRejectedExecutionHandler(new ThreadPoolExecutor.CallerRunsPolicy());
        executor.initialize();
        return executor;
    }
    
    @Bean(name = "aggregatorExecutor")
    public ThreadPoolTaskExecutor aggregatorExecutor() {
        ThreadPoolTaskExecutor executor = new ThreadPoolTaskExecutor();
        executor.setCorePoolSize(20);
        executor.setMaxPoolSize(100);
        executor.setQueueCapacity(200);
        executor.setThreadNamePrefix("aggregator-");
        executor.initialize();
        return executor;
    }
}
Key Takeaways
Orchestration when you need: Request-response, complex logic, easy debugging

Choreography when you need: Loose coupling, scalability, event sourcing

Aggregation for: Read-side optimization, dashboards, data composition

Saga for: Distributed transactions, compensating actions

Pro tip: Don't choose one pattern exclusively. Use hybrid approach - orchestrate critical path, choreograph side effects, aggregate for reads!

This gives you complete control over how services collaborate while maintaining loose coupling where needed.

How to achieve data consistency across microservices?
Read 10 web pages
Achieving data consistency across microservices is a fundamental challenge because you're moving from a single database with ACID transactions to multiple services with their own databases. The key is accepting that distributed systems require different consistency models than monolithic applications.

The Core Problem: Why Traditional Transactions Don't Work
In a monolithic application, you can wrap multiple database operations in a single @Transactional method. If anything fails, everything rolls back atomically.

In a microservices architecture, a single business operation typically spans multiple services:

text
Create Order → Reserve Inventory → Process Payment → Create Shipment
If payment fails after inventory is reserved, you cannot simply "rollback" across services. The inventory service has already committed its changes in its own database.

Pattern 1: Eventual Consistency with Async Messaging
Best for: Most business scenarios where immediate consistency isn't critical

How It Works
Services communicate asynchronously via message brokers (Kafka, RabbitMQ). Data becomes consistent "eventually" rather than immediately.

Implementation with Spring Boot + Kafka
java
// Order Service - Publishes event after local transaction
@Service
@Slf4j
public class OrderService {
    
    @Autowired
    private OrderRepository orderRepository;
    
    @Autowired
    private KafkaTemplate<String, Object> kafkaTemplate;
    
    @Transactional
    public Order createOrder(CreateOrderRequest request) {
        // 1. Save to local database (commits immediately)
        Order order = new Order(request);
        order.setStatus(OrderStatus.PENDING);
        Order saved = orderRepository.save(order);
        
        // 2. Publish event (will be consumed by other services)
        OrderCreatedEvent event = new OrderCreatedEvent(
            saved.getId(), 
            request.getProductId(), 
            request.getQuantity()
        );
        kafkaTemplate.send("order.events", event);
        
        return saved;
    }
}

// Inventory Service - Consumes event and updates its own data
@Component
@Slf4j
public class InventoryEventHandler {
    
    @Autowired
    private InventoryRepository inventoryRepository;
    
    @KafkaListener(topics = "order.events", groupId = "inventory-group")
    public void handleOrderCreated(OrderCreatedEvent event) {
        // Updates inventory in its own database
        // This happens AFTER order service commits
        inventoryRepository.reserveStock(
            event.getProductId(), 
            event.getQuantity()
        );
    }
}
Trade-offs:

✅ High availability and scalability

✅ Loose coupling between services

❌ Temporary inconsistency (window between event publish and consumption)

Pattern 2: Saga Pattern (Distributed Transactions)
Best for: Business workflows where you need coordinated rollback across services

A Saga is a sequence of local transactions with compensating actions. If a step fails, compensating transactions undo previous steps.

Choreography-Based Saga (Decentralized)
Services communicate via events, and each service knows what to do next.

java
// Order Service - Step 1
@Service
@Slf4j
public class OrderSagaParticipant {
    
    @Autowired
    private KafkaTemplate<String, Object> kafkaTemplate;
    
    @Transactional
    public Order createOrder(CreateOrderRequest request) {
        Order order = orderRepository.save(new Order(request));
        
        // Publish event to trigger next step (Inventory)
        kafkaTemplate.send("saga.order.created", 
            new OrderCreatedEvent(order.getId(), request.getProductId(), request.getQuantity()));
        
        return order;
    }
    
    // Compensation method - called if later steps fail
    @KafkaListener(topics = "saga.order.compensate")
    public void compensateOrder(OrderCompensationEvent event) {
        Order order = orderRepository.findById(event.getOrderId())
            .orElseThrow();
        order.setStatus(OrderStatus.CANCELLED);
        orderRepository.save(order);
        log.info("Order compensated: {}", event.getOrderId());
    }
}

// Inventory Service - Step 2
@Service
@Slf4j
public class InventorySagaParticipant {
    
    @KafkaListener(topics = "saga.order.created")
    public void handleOrderCreated(OrderCreatedEvent event) {
        try {
            // Attempt to reserve stock
            boolean reserved = inventoryService.reserveStock(
                event.getProductId(), 
                event.getQuantity()
            );
            
            if (reserved) {
                // Success - trigger next step (Payment)
                kafkaTemplate.send("saga.inventory.reserved", 
                    new InventoryReservedEvent(event.getOrderId()));
            } else {
                // Failure - trigger compensation for previous steps
                kafkaTemplate.send("saga.order.compensate", 
                    new OrderCompensationEvent(event.getOrderId()));
            }
        } catch (Exception e) {
            // Failure - trigger compensation
            kafkaTemplate.send("saga.order.compensate", 
                new OrderCompensationEvent(event.getOrderId()));
        }
    }
    
    // Compensation method
    @KafkaListener(topics = "saga.inventory.compensate")
    public void compensateInventory(InventoryCompensationEvent event) {
        inventoryService.releaseStock(event.getProductId(), event.getQuantity());
        log.info("Inventory compensation completed");
    }
}

// Payment Service - Step 3
@Service
@Slf4j
public class PaymentSagaParticipant {
    
    @KafkaListener(topics = "saga.inventory.reserved")
    public void handleInventoryReserved(InventoryReservedEvent event) {
        try {
            paymentService.processPayment(event.getOrderId());
            
            // Success - all steps complete
            kafkaTemplate.send("saga.payment.completed", 
                new PaymentCompletedEvent(event.getOrderId()));
                
        } catch (Exception e) {
            // Payment failed - trigger compensation for inventory AND order
            kafkaTemplate.send("saga.inventory.compensate", 
                new InventoryCompensationEvent(event.getOrderId()));
            kafkaTemplate.send("saga.order.compensate", 
                new OrderCompensationEvent(event.getOrderId()));
        }
    }
}
Orchestration-Based Saga (Centralized)
A central orchestrator manages the entire workflow:

java
@RestController
@RequestMapping("/saga")
public class SagaOrchestratorController {
    
    private final RestTemplate restTemplate = new RestTemplate();
    
    @PostMapping("/order")
    public String placeOrder(@RequestParam String product, @RequestParam double amount) {
        
        // Step 1: Create Order
        Object order = restTemplate.postForObject(
            "http://localhost:8081/orders?product=" + product,
            null, Object.class
        );
        
        if (order == null) {
            return "Order creation failed";
        }
        
        // Step 2: Reserve Inventory
        Boolean inventory = restTemplate.postForObject(
            "http://localhost:8082/inventory/reserve?product=" + product,
            null, Boolean.class
        );
        
        if (inventory == null || !inventory) {
            return "Inventory reservation failed";
        }
        
        // Step 3: Process Payment
        Boolean payment = restTemplate.postForObject(
            "http://localhost:8083/payments/pay?amount=" + amount,
            null, Boolean.class
        );
        
        if (payment == null || !payment) {
            // Payment failed - compensate previous steps
            restTemplate.postForObject(
                "http://localhost:8082/inventory/release?product=" + product,
                null, Void.class
            );
            return "Payment failed, transaction rolled back";
        }
        
        return "Order completed successfully";
    }
}
Saga Advantages:

✅ Enables distributed transactions without 2PC

✅ Maintains business-level consistency

✅ Services remain loosely coupled

Saga Disadvantages:

❌ More complex to implement

❌ Only eventual consistency (not real-time)

❌ Requires designing compensating transactions for every step

Pattern 3: Transactional Outbox Pattern
Best for: Guaranteeing event delivery when a service both updates its database and publishes events

The "dual write" problem occurs when a service needs to both update its database and send a message. If one fails, data becomes inconsistent.

Implementation
java
// Entity for Outbox table
@Entity
@Table(name = "outbox")
@Data
public class OutboxEvent {
    @Id
    @GeneratedValue
    private UUID id;
    
    private UUID aggregateId;
    private String eventType;
    private String payload;
    private LocalDateTime createdAt;
    private boolean published;
    private LocalDateTime publishedAt;
}

// Order Service - Writes to both business table AND outbox in SAME transaction
@Service
@Slf4j
public class OrderServiceWithOutbox {
    
    @Autowired
    private OrderRepository orderRepository;
    
    @Autowired
    private OutboxRepository outboxRepository;
    
    @Transactional  // Single transaction covers both writes!
    public Order createOrder(CreateOrderRequest request) {
        // 1. Save business data
        Order order = new Order(request);
        Order saved = orderRepository.save(order);
        
        // 2. Save event to outbox table (same transaction)
        OutboxEvent outboxEvent = new OutboxEvent();
        outboxEvent.setAggregateId(saved.getId());
        outboxEvent.setEventType("ORDER_CREATED");
        outboxEvent.setPayload(serializeEvent(saved));
        outboxEvent.setCreatedAt(LocalDateTime.now());
        outboxEvent.setPublished(false);
        
        outboxRepository.save(outboxEvent);
        
        return saved;  // Transaction commits BOTH writes atomically
    }
}

// Poller Service - Separate process that publishes events from outbox
@Component
@Slf4j
public class OutboxPoller {
    
    @Autowired
    private OutboxRepository outboxRepository;
    
    @Autowired
    private KafkaTemplate<String, String> kafkaTemplate;
    
    @Scheduled(fixedDelay = 5000)  // Poll every 5 seconds
    @Transactional
    public void publishEvents() {
        List<OutboxEvent> unpublishedEvents = outboxRepository
            .findByPublishedFalse(PageRequest.of(0, 100));
        
        for (OutboxEvent event : unpublishedEvents) {
            try {
                // Publish to Kafka
                kafkaTemplate.send("order.events", event.getPayload()).get(5, TimeUnit.SECONDS);
                
                // Mark as published
                event.setPublished(true);
                event.setPublishedAt(LocalDateTime.now());
                outboxRepository.save(event);
                
                log.info("Published event: {}", event.getId());
                
            } catch (Exception e) {
                log.error("Failed to publish event: {}", event.getId(), e);
                // Will retry on next poll
            }
        }
    }
}
This pattern ensures at-least-once delivery without dual-write inconsistency.

Pattern 4: Idempotency for Safe Retries
Best for: Preventing duplicate processing when messages are retried

When using message queues, the same message might be delivered multiple times. Idempotent operations ensure processing a message twice has the same effect as processing it once.

java
@Entity
@Table(name = "processed_events")
@Data
public class ProcessedEvent {
    @Id
    private String eventId;
    private LocalDateTime processedAt;
}

@Component
@Slf4j
public class IdempotentEventHandler {
    
    @Autowired
    private ProcessedEventRepository processedEventRepository;
    
    @Autowired
    private InventoryService inventoryService;
    
    @KafkaListener(topics = "order.events", groupId = "inventory-group")
    public void handleOrderCreated(ConsumerRecord<String, String> record, Acknowledgment ack) {
        String eventId = record.headers().lastHeader("event-id").value().toString();
        
        // Check if already processed
        if (processedEventRepository.existsById(eventId)) {
            log.info("Duplicate event detected, skipping: {}", eventId);
            ack.acknowledge();
            return;
        }
        
        try {
            OrderCreatedEvent event = deserialize(record.value());
            
            // Business logic - this should be idempotent
            // e.g., setting stock = 100 is idempotent, stock = stock - 1 is NOT
            inventoryService.setStockLevel(event.getProductId(), event.getQuantity());
            
            // Mark as processed
            ProcessedEvent processed = new ProcessedEvent();
            processed.setEventId(eventId);
            processed.setProcessedAt(LocalDateTime.now());
            processedEventRepository.save(processed);
            
            ack.acknowledge();
            
        } catch (Exception e) {
            log.error("Error processing event", e);
            // Don't acknowledge - will retry
        }
    }
}
Pattern 5: CQRS (Command Query Responsibility Segregation)
Best for: Complex read models that aggregate data from multiple services

Separate write operations (commands) from read operations (queries). Writes go to service-specific databases; reads come from a denormalized read database.

java
// Command Side - Order Service handles writes
@RestController
@RequestMapping("/api/orders")
public class OrderCommandController {
    
    @PostMapping
    public ResponseEntity<Order> createOrder(@RequestBody CreateOrderRequest request) {
        Order order = orderService.createOrder(request);
        
        // Publish event for read model updates
        kafkaTemplate.send("order.events", new OrderCreatedEvent(order));
        
        return ResponseEntity.accepted().body(order);
    }
}

// Query Side - Dedicated service for reads
@Service
@Slf4j
public class OrderQueryService {
    
    @Autowired
    private OrderReadRepository orderReadRepository;  // Separate database
    
    @KafkaListener(topics = "order.events")
    public void updateReadModel(Object event) {
        if (event instanceof OrderCreatedEvent) {
            OrderCreatedEvent e = (OrderCreatedEvent) event;
            
            // Denormalized data from multiple services
            OrderReadModel model = new OrderReadModel();
            model.setOrderId(e.getOrderId());
            model.setCustomerName(getCustomerName(e.getCustomerId()));  // From Customer Service
            model.setProductName(getProductName(e.getProductId()));      // From Product Service
            model.setTotalAmount(e.getTotalAmount());
            model.setStatus("PENDING");
            
            orderReadRepository.save(model);
        }
    }
}

// Query API - Fast reads from denormalized database
@RestController
@RequestMapping("/api/query/orders")
public class OrderQueryController {
    
    @GetMapping("/{orderId}")
    public OrderReadModel getOrder(@PathVariable UUID orderId) {
        // Direct database read - no service calls!
        return orderReadRepository.findById(orderId)
            .orElseThrow(() -> new ResourceNotFoundException("Order not found"));
    }
}
Pattern Comparison & Selection Guide
Pattern	Consistency	Complexity	Best For
Eventual Consistency	Weak	Low	Non-critical updates, high scale needs
Saga (Choreography)	Eventual	Medium	Decoupled workflows, independent teams
Saga (Orchestration)	Eventual	Medium-High	Complex workflows needing central visibility
Transactional Outbox	Strong (delivery)	Medium	Reliable event publishing
Idempotency	N/A	Low	Safe retries, preventing duplicates
CQRS	Eventual	High	Complex read models, reporting
Practical Recommendation for Java 8 + Spring Boot
Start with Eventual Consistency + Idempotency for most use cases. Add Saga only for business-critical workflows that require coordinated rollback.

Maven Dependencies:

xml
<dependencies>
    <!-- Spring Kafka -->
    <dependency>
        <groupId>org.springframework.kafka</groupId>
        <artifactId>spring-kafka</artifactId>
    </dependency>
    
    <!-- Resilience4j for retry -->
    <dependency>
        <groupId>io.github.resilience4j</groupId>
        <artifactId>resilience4j-spring-boot2</artifactId>
        <version>1.7.1</version>
    </dependency>
    
    <!-- For Outbox pattern -->
    <dependency>
        <groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>
        <artifactId>spring-boot-starter-data-jpa</artifactId>
    </dependency>
</dependencies>
Key Takeaways:

Accept eventual consistency - it's not a bug, it's a feature of distributed systems

Design compensating transactions for every business operation that spans services

Use idempotency keys to safely retry failed operations

Implement the Outbox pattern when you need guaranteed event delivery

Never use 2PC (two-phase commit) in microservices - it kills scalability

The reality is that strong consistency across services is rarely necessary for business applications. Most systems can tolerate brief periods of inconsistency (seconds or minutes) as long as they eventually become consistent. Focus on designing your system to handle temporary inconsistencies gracefully rather than fighting the distributed nature of microservices.

How to centralise access to microservice using an api gateway?
Read 9 web pages
Centralizing access to microservices through an API Gateway is a fundamental pattern in microservice architectures. In the context of a Java 8 and Spring Boot stack, Spring Cloud Gateway is the modern, recommended solution for this task .

Think of an API Gateway as the single entry point for all client applications (web, mobile, IoT, etc.) . It sits between the clients and your backend services, acting as a reverse proxy to route requests to the appropriate microservice . This approach abstracts your internal microservice landscape, providing a single, clean API to the outside world and centralizing cross-cutting concerns.

💡 The Benefits of Using an API Gateway
Implementing this pattern offers several key advantages:

Simplified Client Logic: Clients no longer need to know the locations of individual services (e.g., http://localhost:8081, http://localhost:8082). They interact with a single, well-known endpoint (http://your-api-gateway.com) .

Centralized Cross-Cutting Concerns: It becomes the ideal place to enforce common functionality like authentication, authorization, rate limiting, request logging, and response caching for all your services .

Improved Security: By hiding your internal microservices behind the gateway, you create a "demilitarized zone" (DMZ) . You can expose only the gateway to the internet, keeping your internal services on a private network for a more secure topology .

Protocol Translation and Flexibility: The gateway can translate between different protocols (e.g., accepting external HTTP requests and translating them to gRPC or AMQP internally) and allows you to refactor or change the location of internal services without impacting clients .

🏗️ Setting Up an API Gateway with Spring Cloud Gateway
Here is a practical, step-by-step guide to building your own API Gateway using Java 8 and Spring Boot.

Step 1: Create a New Spring Boot Project
Create a new Spring Boot project. While the provided example uses Maven, the same dependencies can be added to a build.gradle file. You need to include the spring-cloud-starter-gateway module. It's important to note that Spring Cloud Gateway is built on Spring WebFlux and uses Netty by default, not the traditional Servlet-based web starter (spring-boot-starter-web) .

xml
<dependencies>
    <!-- Core Spring Cloud Gateway Starter -->
    <dependency>
        <groupId>org.springframework.cloud</groupId>
        <artifactId>spring-cloud-starter-gateway</artifactId>
    </dependency>
    <!-- Optional: For service discovery integration -->
    <dependency>
        <groupId>org.springframework.cloud</groupId>
        <artifactId>spring-cloud-starter-netflix-eureka-client</artifactId>
    </dependency>
    <!-- Optional: To expose gateway routes endpoint -->
    <dependency>
        <groupId>org.springframework.boot</groupId>
        <artifactId>spring-boot-starter-actuator</artifactId>
    </dependency>
</dependencies>
Setting up dependencies for a new Gateway service project 

Step 2: Configure Routes (The Core Function)
Routing is the most basic function of the gateway, defined by an ID, a destination URI, a set of predicates (conditions to match a request), and filters (to modify the request/response) .

You can configure routes using YAML or Java. The YAML approach in application.yml is clean and common.

This configuration defines two simple routes. Requests starting with /users are routed to http://localhost:8081, and requests starting with /orders go to http://localhost:8082 .

yaml
spring:
  cloud:
    gateway:
      routes:
        - id: user-service-route
          uri: http://localhost:8081
          predicates:
            - Path=/users/**
        - id: order-service-route
          uri: http://localhost:8082
          predicates:
            - Path=/orders/**
Example of basic route configuration using YAML 

Step 3: Make Routes More Dynamic
For a more robust setup, integrate Spring Cloud Gateway with a Service Registry like Netflix Eureka . This allows the gateway to dynamically discover service instances without hardcoding their network locations.

Enable the discovery locator by adding these properties to your application.yml .

yaml
spring:
  cloud:
    gateway:
      discovery:
        locator:
          enabled: true
          lower-case-service-id: true

eureka:
  client:
    serviceUrl:
      defaultZone: http://localhost:8761/eureka/
Configuration for enabling dynamic routing based on Eureka service discovery 

When this is enabled, if your user-service registers with Eureka, the gateway can automatically create a route for it, typically at http://<GATEWAY-HOST>/user-service/**. This decouples the gateway's configuration from the specific network addresses of your services.

Step 4: Enhance Routes with Filters
Filters are where the gateway's true power for centralizing logic shines. They can modify requests and responses . Here are a few common examples.

StripPrefix Filter: This is useful when your gateway path includes a context that your backend service doesn't expect. The StripPrefix=1 filter removes the first segment (/users) from the path before forwarding the request to lb://user-service .

yaml
spring:
  cloud:
    gateway:
      routes:
        - id: user-service
          uri: lb://user-service # 'lb' indicates load balancing using service discovery
          predicates:
            - Path=/users/**
          filters:
            - StripPrefix=1
Example of using the StripPrefix filter to modify the request path before forwarding 

AddRequestHeader Filter: This filter adds a custom HTTP header to the request as it is proxied to the downstream service .

yaml
filters:
  - AddRequestHeader=X-Request-Foo, Bar
Example of using a filter to add a custom request header

Customizing Paths with RewritePath: You can use a filter with a regular expression to rewrite the request path more flexibly .

yaml
filters:
  - RewritePath=/api/v1/users/(?<segment>.*), /$\{segment}
Example of using the RewritePath filter to change the request path structure 

Step 5: Hide Your Services for Security
The "hidden network" pattern is a key security benefit. Using Docker or a similar network overlay, you can configure your production environment so that only the API Gateway has a public IP or is accessible from the internet. All internal services, including the registry and the business microservices themselves, are placed on an internal network with no public access .

A user request flows like this :

A client makes a request to the public gateway endpoint (e.g., http://public-gateway.com/service/greeting).

The gateway, based on its configuration, forwards the request to the internal service (e.g., http://greeting-service.internal:8080/greeting).

The internal service processes the request and sends a response back to the gateway.

The gateway relays that response to the original client.

💎 Summary and Key Takeaways
Here are the most important points to remember when centralizing microservice access with an API Gateway:

Modern Choice: For a Java 8 and Spring Boot stack, Spring Cloud Gateway is the standard, non-blocking, and highly performant choice for building an API Gateway .

Core Mechanism: The gateway operates on routes, which are composed of a destination URI, predicates (matching rules), and filters (modification logic) .

Dynamic Routing: Integrating with a Service Registry (like Netflix Eureka) allows the gateway to dynamically discover service locations, making your architecture more resilient to change .

Centralization for Security: The API Gateway is the perfect enforcement point for cross-cutting concerns like authentication, authorization, rate limiting, and logging, and it enables the "hidden network" pattern for a secure topology .

Avoid Business Logic: Remember that the API Gateway is a utility component. Its primary role is routing and infrastructure-level concerns. It should not contain business logic, as this can lead to tight coupling and maintenance issues .

I hope this guide provides a clear path forward for your architecture. Do you have a specific use case, like integrating security or setting up rate limiting, that you would like to explore further?

How to split monolithic db across microservices?
Read 10 web pages
Splitting a monolithic database is the most technically challenging part of moving to microservices. The core problem is that monoliths often have a single, shared database where tables are heavily interconnected via foreign keys and joins.

The goal is to move from a shared database to a Database per Service model, where each microservice fully owns its data and no other service can access it directly. This guide provides a step-by-step, low-risk strategy to achieve this decomposition.

🗺️ The Decomposition Roadmap
The following roadmap outlines the key phases for splitting your database, starting with analysis and ending with a fully decomposed architecture.








🔍 Phase 1: Analyze and Plan the Split
Before changing anything, you must understand your existing database structure.

Map Table Ownership and Dependencies: Your first task is to categorize every table in your monolith. Determine which business domain (and thus, which future microservice) should own it. You also need a complete map of how tables are coupled.

Implementation Coupling: Tables are tightly linked via foreign keys. A change to the products table might break the orders table.

Domain Coupling: A single table serves multiple business domains. A users table might be used by Sales, Support, and Billing domains, each with different needs.

Tooling: Use database analysis tools like SchemaSpy to generate visual dependency graphs. For a more data-driven approach, you can export your schema into a graph database to run algorithms that identify natural clusters or "seams" in your data.

Identify Bounded Contexts: Look for groups of tables that are highly cohesive (they change together for the same business reason) but have low coupling (few dependencies on tables outside the group). These groups are your candidates for initial extraction. This approach aligns with Domain-Driven Design (DDD) principles.

🏗️ Phase 2: Refactor Within the Monolith
This is the most critical, and often most difficult, phase. You will restructure the existing monolith to break its hard database dependencies, without changing the application's external behavior. This internal reorganization creates the "seams" needed for extraction.

Break Foreign Key Relationships: In a microservices architecture, you cannot have database-enforced foreign keys between services, as this would create a tight, unbreakable coupling. You must replace them with application-level references.

Action: Remove the REFERENCES clause from table definitions.

Keep the Reference: The columns (e.g., user_id in the orders table) stay, but as a simple integer, not a foreign key. Referential integrity is now the responsibility of the services, not the database.

Create API Facades: Introduce internal facades (APIs) within the monolith to handle all read and write operations for a specific domain (e.g., the Product Catalog).

Why: This breaks direct database joins. Instead of the Orders module joining the Products table, it will call the ProductCatalogQueryFacade.

Result: You create a defined, logical interface that can later be replaced by calls to an external microservice.

🚚 Phase 3: Introduce New Databases and Sync Data
Once the internal facades are in place, you can create the new, independent databases.

Create New Databases: For your first target domain (e.g., product-service), create a brand new, empty database instance.

Implement the Outbox Pattern: To reliably sync data from the monolith's database to the new service's database without using fragile dual-writes, implement the Transactional Outbox pattern.

How it Works: When the monolith updates the products table, it saves the event (e.g., ProductUpdated) to an outbox table within the same database transaction. A separate process (a "poller") reads this outbox table and publishes the event to a message broker like Kafka or RabbitMQ. The new product-service consumes the event and updates its own database. This guarantees at-least-once delivery.

Backfill Existing Data: Perform a one-time backfill to copy all existing product data from the monolith into the new service's database. The timing of this backfill is critical. The safest approach is to do it after you've started the dual-write process described in the next step, ensuring no updates are missed.

🚦 Phase 4: Incrementally Migrate with the Strangler Fig Pattern
This is where you safely switch traffic over. The Strangler Fig pattern is the industry standard for this.

Dual Writes (The Update Duplicator): Introduce a component (e.g., UpdateDuplicator) that intercepts all calls to your ProductCatalogUpdateFacade and writes the changes to both the monolith's database and the new external product-service via its API. The monolith remains the source of truth for reads.

Dual Reads (The Query Toggler): Introduce a QueryToggler component that uses a feature flag to decide whether to serve a read request from the monolith's internal facade or from the new external product-service.

Canary Testing: You can start by routing 1% of your most tolerant users to the new service. If everything works, you increase the percentage to 5%, then 25%, and so on. This allows you to test the new service with live traffic while minimizing risk.

Switch Over: Once you're confident, flip the feature flag to route 100% of reads and writes to the new service. The monolith's internal tables for that domain are now "dead" (no longer being written to, and not being read from).

🧹 Phase 5: Finalize and Clean Up
This is the victory lap. With all traffic going to the new service, you can safely remove the old code and data.

Delete Code: Remove the old internal facades, the UpdateDuplicator, and the QueryToggler from the monolith's codebase.

Delete Tables: Drop the now-redundant tables (e.g., products, categories) from the monolithic database.

Celebrate: You have successfully decomposed a part of your monolith! Repeat the process for the next domain.

⚖️ Handling Cross-Service Data Consistency
Once the database is split, you lose the ability to use database transactions (ACID) across services. For example, creating an Order and deducting Inventory is no longer a single, atomic operation. Use the Saga pattern for distributed transactions. A saga is a sequence of local transactions where each step publishes an event that triggers the next step. If a step fails, the saga runs compensating transactions to undo the previous steps.

🚀 Final Checklist for Your Decomposition
Here is a summary of the steps you will take:

Analyzed your monolithic database to identify dependencies and bounded contexts.

Broken all direct foreign keys between tables owned by different domains.

Created internal API facades for reading and writing data within the monolith.

Implemented a message broker (like Kafka) for reliable event distribution.

Built a new, empty database for your first target service.

Used the Outbox pattern to reliably sync data from the monolith to the new service.

Introduced a feature-flag-driven toggler to route read requests.

Gradually shifted traffic from the monolith's database to the new service.

Removed the old code and database tables for the extracted service.

I hope this phased, low-risk roadmap gives you a clear path forward. Splitting a database is a significant but manageable undertaking when broken down into these steps. Which part of this process would you like to explore in more detail? For instance, we could dive deeper into the Outbox pattern or the specifics of implementing a Saga.

Mow to make microservices more resilient using circuit breaker with open, half open and closed state hands on and diagram with complet definition and yaml configurations?
Circuit Breaker Pattern: Complete Hands-On Guide
The Circuit Breaker pattern prevents cascading failures in distributed systems. When a service fails repeatedly, the circuit breaker "trips" (opens) and stops sending requests, allowing the failing service time to recover.

📊 Circuit Breaker States - Complete Definition
text
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                         CIRCUIT BREAKER STATES                          │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

                                    ┌─────────────────┐
                                    │                 │
                            ┌──────▶│    CLOSED        │◀──────┐
                            │       │  (Normal Flow)   │       │
                            │       │                 │       │
                            │       └────────┬────────┘       │
                            │                 │               │
                      Success/              Failure           │
                      Reset                 Count >          │
                                            Threshold         │
                            │                 │               │
                            │                 ▼               │
                            │       ┌─────────────────┐       │
                            │       │                 │       │
                            │       │     OPEN        │       │
                            │       │  (Reject Calls) │       │
                            │       │                 │       │
                            │       └────────┬────────┘       │
                            │                 │               │
                            │           Timeout               │
                            │           Expires               │
                            │                 │               │
                            │                 ▼               │
                            │       ┌─────────────────┐       │
                            │       │                 │       │
                            └──────│   HALF-OPEN      │───────┘
                                   │ (Test Recovery)  │
                                   │                 │
                                   └─────────────────┘
State Definitions
State	Definition	Behavior
CLOSED	Normal operation. Circuit is "closed" allowing requests to flow through.	All requests pass through. Failures are counted. When failure count/threshold reached → transitions to OPEN.
OPEN	Circuit is "tripped". Requests fail immediately without attempting the call.	Requests fail fast with fallback. No calls to dependent service. After timeout duration → transitions to HALF-OPEN.
HALF-OPEN	Testing if service has recovered. Limited requests allowed through.	If successful requests exceed threshold → transitions to CLOSED. If any failure occurs → transitions back to OPEN.
🛠️ Hands-On Implementation with Resilience4j
Step 1: Dependencies (build.gradle)
gradle
plugins {
    id 'org.springframework.boot' version '2.7.18'
    id 'io.spring.dependency-management' version '1.0.15.RELEASE'
    id 'java'
}

group = 'com.example'
version = '1.0.0'
sourceCompatibility = '1.8'

repositories {
    mavenCentral()
}

ext {
    set('springCloudVersion', "2021.0.8")
    set('resilience4jVersion', "1.7.1")
}

dependencies {
    // Spring Boot Starters
    implementation 'org.springframework.boot:spring-boot-starter-web'
    implementation 'org.springframework.boot:spring-boot-starter-actuator'
    implementation 'org.springframework.boot:spring-boot-starter-aop'
    
    // Resilience4j
    implementation "io.github.resilience4j:resilience4j-spring-boot2:${resilience4jVersion}"
    implementation "io.github.resilience4j:resilience4j-circuitbreaker:${resilience4jVersion}"
    implementation "io.github.resilience4j:resilience4j-timelimiter:${resilience4jVersion}"
    
    // Micrometer for metrics
    implementation 'io.micrometer:micrometer-registry-prometheus'
    
    // Feign Client (for service-to-service calls)
    implementation 'org.springframework.cloud:spring-cloud-starter-openfeign'
    
    // Lombok
    compileOnly 'org.projectlombok:lombok'
    annotationProcessor 'org.projectlombok:lombok'
    
    // Testing
    testImplementation 'org.springframework.boot:spring-boot-starter-test'
